## September 9, 2026 — Media URL Cleanup: convert hardcoded absolute download URLs to relative paths

Lauren spotted (via a screenshot of a Resource's "Download Files" meta box) that several `cropx_resource` Download File fields still contained `http://ec2-100-25-145-190.compute-1.amazonaws.com/wp-content/uploads/...` — the site's old EC2 staging address — baked in as plain text, even though the install now lives at new.cropx.com. She asked whether this could be fixed site-wide, and said she'd prefer relative links over hardcoding any domain (rather than just swapping in the current domain and risking the same bug again at the eventual real launch-domain cutover).

**Root cause:** the "Download Files" meta box (and the single download-URL field on `cropx_publication`) stores whatever URL WordPress's Media Library picker returns *at the moment an editor clicks it* — a plain string in postmeta, not an attachment ID. `wp.media`'s JS picker itself is fine (`wp_prepare_attachment_for_js()` always computes the URL from the *current* site's upload path, so picks made today already resolve to new.cropx.com correctly) — the stale values are just old data, picked back when the install was still reachable at the EC2 address, and nothing ever goes back and re-resolves an already-saved plain-text URL. Confirmed via grep that only two content types store raw URL strings like this: `cropx_resource` (`download_url`, `download_url_letter`, `download_url_a4`, `download_url_{es,pt,fr,de,nl,ro,ru}_a4`, `guide_url_en` — 11 fields per resource) and `cropx_publication` (`pub_download_url`, one field). Team member LinkedIn URLs and Dealer website URLs are also raw strings but point at external domains, so they're unaffected by this migration.

**Fix:** built `inc/relative-media-urls.php` — a new Tools → "Media URL Cleanup" admin page (manage_options only). It scans every `cropx_resource` and `cropx_publication` post for exactly those known meta keys, and for any value starting with either the dead EC2 address or the site's own current `home_url()` (http or https), strips the domain down to a root-relative path (e.g. `/wp-content/uploads/2026/08/file.pdf`). A root-relative path resolves correctly against whatever domain is currently serving the page, so this can't recur at the next domain change (e.g. the eventual new.cropx.com → cropx.com cutover) — matches Lauren's stated preference over hardcoding a replacement domain. "Preview changes" runs read-only and shows a before/after table; "Apply changes" saves. Safe to re-run — an already-relative value is left untouched. Deliberately scoped to just these two CPTs' known fields rather than a site-wide search-and-replace, so it can't touch `wp_options` (siteurl/home) or anything else.

Wired into `functions.php` behind the same `file_exists()` guard used for other small patches (WP File Manager's zip extraction has a documented history of dropping files — see "WordPress block development gotchas" below). No `CROPX_THEME_VERSION` bump — PHP-only, nothing cached.

**Files changed:** `inc/relative-media-urls.php` (new), `functions.php` (added guarded require).
**Delivered:** `media-url-cleanup-patch.zip` (new file + updated functions.php) and `media-url-cleanup-revert-backup.zip` (original functions.php only — the new include file just needs deleting to fully revert, since functions.php guards its absence).

**Not yet done / flagged to Lauren:** this only fixes the two known CPTs' meta fields. If a stray absolute EC2 link is also pasted somewhere in a page's body content (not just these postmeta fields), a dedicated search-and-replace plugin (e.g. Better Search Replace) would be the right broader tool — recommended to Lauren as a follow-up but not installed by me (requires a WP.org plugin install, which is her call). Also: `php -l` wasn't available in this sandbox to lint the new file automatically — reviewed manually for brace/syntax balance instead; worth a quick look at the Tools page after deploy to confirm no fatal error.

## September 9, 2026 — Logo Carousel: serve properly-sized logo images (fixes remaining "Improve image delivery" waste)

Follow-up to the hero-photo responsive-image fix (below) and the fresh PageSpeed re-run Lauren did after installing it. Desktop score is now 90 (TBT 0ms, LCP 1.6s), and "Improve image delivery" dropped from 793 KiB to 434 KiB — the hero fix is confirmed working. Most of what's left in that 434 KiB is the Logo Carousel: customer logos were being served at their full uploaded resolution (400×400 in the flagged case) into a 120×120 display slot (`.ls-logo-slot` in `logo-strip/style.css`), the same "larger than it needs to be for its displayed dimensions" pattern as the hero photos, just smaller in absolute terms (~5-9 KiB per logo).

**Fix:** added `cropx_logo_img_url( $attachment_id, $fallback_url )` to `inc/helpers.php`. It requests WordPress's built-in `'medium'` intermediate size (300px max dimension, proportional — never cropped) instead of the full original, and falls back to the original URL for anything without an attachment ID (the hardcoded default SVG logo set) or for SVGs specifically (vector — no smaller raster rendition exists, so there's nothing to gain by trying). `'medium'` was chosen over a custom-registered size because it already exists for every previously-uploaded logo with zero extra setup — no `add_image_size()` registration and no thumbnail-regeneration step Lauren would have to run, unlike a purpose-built size would need. 300px is comfortably larger than the 120px display size for 2x/3x retina, while still a real reduction from most uploads' native resolution.

Wired into `logo-strip/render.php`: the block renders each logo twice (the marquee duplicates its logo set for the seamless CSS scroll loop), so the resolved src/dims are now computed once per logo per pass and reused across both the linked and unlinked `<img>` markup, rather than re-deriving it inline four times. Also switched `cropx_img_dims_attr()` to receive the real attachment ID (previously always passed `0` and relied on `attachment_url_to_postid()` guessing from the URL) — same visual result (dims are read from the original's stored metadata either way, since aspect ratio doesn't change across sizes) but a more direct, reliable lookup.

**No `CROPX_THEME_VERSION` bump** — same reasoning as the hero-photo patch: this only touches PHP (`render.php` + `helpers.php`), no cached CSS/JS output changed.

**Files changed:** `inc/helpers.php` (new `cropx_logo_img_url()` function), `src/blocks/logo-strip/render.php`, plus the matching `build/blocks/logo-strip/render.php`.

**Delivered two zips**: `logo-carousel-image-size-patch.zip` (forward — `inc/helpers.php` + `build/blocks/logo-strip/`) and `logo-carousel-image-size-revert-backup.zip` (byte-verified pre-fix backup, same file set). Deploy: upload the patch zip into `wp-content/themes/cropx/` via WP File Manager and extract.

**Next step (Lauren's plan):** wait a few hours for ShortPixel's CDN to fully settle (the "Use efficient cache lifetimes" finding is still showing partial savings — 1,740 KiB as of the last run, down from 2,887 KiB — even though a direct header check confirms the CDN is genuinely serving `max-age=31535635` right now; likely just propagation catching up across the full asset set) before re-running PageSpeed again, to avoid drawing conclusions from numbers that are still mid-transition. Render-blocking requests (#3 on the priority list) remains deliberately untouched — desktop's only showing ~430-490ms of estimated savings against an already-strong 90/100 score with 0ms TBT, and the last attempt at this caused a real regression, so it's on hold pending a fresh look (including mobile, which is where the previous regression actually showed up) rather than being worth the risk for a sub-500ms desktop win.

## September 9, 2026 — Hero photos: added responsive `srcset`/`sizes` (fixes PageSpeed "Improve image delivery")

Lauren shared a fresh PageSpeed run (both mobile and desktop) after the ShortPixel CDN/caching work (see below) was confirmed working. One finding remained that ShortPixel couldn't fix: "Improve image delivery," 793 KiB potential savings on desktop. Root cause — all 6 hero-family blocks (`hero`, `hero-curved`, `hero-curved-standard`, `hero-curved-animated`, `hero-blog`, `segment-hero`) render their background photo as a single fixed-resolution `<img src="...">` with no `srcset`/`sizes`, so every visitor — phone included — downloaded the same ~2400px original regardless of the ~1330px actual display size. This is a separate, code-level gap from image caching/compression and isn't something a ShortPixel setting can touch.

**Fix:** added `cropx_bg_img_responsive_attr( $attachment_id )` to `inc/helpers.php`, which calls WordPress core's `wp_get_attachment_image_srcset()` to build a `srcset` from WP's own auto-generated intermediate sizes (thumbnail/medium/medium_large/large/full — no custom `add_image_size()` needed), paired with a static `sizes="100vw"`. The static value is safe here because all 6 hero blocks share the same full-bleed CSS pattern confirmed via `style.css` review — the `*-bg` wrapper is `position:absolute; inset:0` and the `<img>` is `width:100%; height:100%; object-fit:cover`, so the photo always spans 100% of the viewport at every breakpoint; there's no split-column layout to account for. Added one line calling the helper right after each block's existing `cropx_img_dims_attr()` call in `render.php`. Returns `''` (no-op) when there's no real attachment ID — covers the hardcoded SVG/PNG fallback illustrations some blocks ship with, which aren't WP attachments and have no intermediate sizes.

**Verification:** all 6 `src/blocks/<name>/render.php` diffs checked against pre-edit backups — each shows exactly one added line. Confirmed via `grep` on `inc/cpts.php` that no hero-specific `add_image_size()` exists (only `cropx-doc-cover` and `cropx-testimonial-avatar`, unrelated), so the srcset draws entirely from WP's default core sizes on every existing photo, no backfill needed. `build/blocks/<name>/render.php` files were `cp`'d directly from the edited `src/` versions rather than run through `npm run build` — safe because `render.php` needs no JS/CSS compilation, wp-scripts just copies it verbatim into `build/`, and this sandbox's webpack pipeline has documented flakiness; re-verified via `diff` that build now exactly matches src for all 6.

**No `CROPX_THEME_VERSION` bump this time** — unlike most patches in this file, this one touches only PHP (`render.php` + `helpers.php`, both server-executed on every request) and outputs no changed CSS/JS. The version-bump convention (CLAUDE.md gotcha #10) exists specifically to bust the browser's cache on `?ver=`-suffixed static assets; PHP templates aren't cached that way, so a bump isn't needed for this fix.

**Files changed:** `inc/helpers.php` (new `cropx_bg_img_responsive_attr()` function), `src/blocks/{hero,hero-curved,hero-curved-standard,hero-curved-animated,hero-blog,segment-hero}/render.php` (one line each), plus the matching `build/blocks/<name>/render.php` for all 6.

**Delivered two zips**: `hero-responsive-image-patch.zip` (forward — `inc/helpers.php` + `build/blocks/` for all 6 hero blocks) and `hero-responsive-revert-backup.zip` (byte-verified pre-fix backup, same file set). Deploy: upload the patch zip into `wp-content/themes/cropx/` via WP File Manager and extract — same small-patch convention as every prior fix in this file.

## September 9, 2026 — Sitewide fix: stale attachment-URL bug across ~28 blocks + starter patterns

Lauren flagged that the Logo Carousel editor-image fix (previous entry) wasn't an isolated case — it's sitewide. Had an agent audit all 49 blocks for the same bug class (an image/file stored as `{ id, url }`, where the raw `url` is used instead of re-resolving fresh from `id`). Confirmed and fixed it everywhere it appeared.

**FRONT-END BUG (visitors could see broken images/dead download links, not just wp-admin) — fixed `render.php` + `edit.js` in all 10:** `segments` (homepage segment panels), `product-sections`, `product-tabs` (card photo + overlay), `video` + `two-column-video` (self-hosted video src), `mid-page-cta` + `pre-footer-cta` (CTA file-download links), and the CTA file-download links in `hero-curved`, `hero-curved-standard`, `hero-curved-animated` (their background/device/phone images were already resolved correctly — only the download CTAs were broken). All now re-resolve from the attachment ID via `wp_get_attachment_url()` / `wp_get_attachment_image_src()` on every render, matching logo-strip's pattern.

**EDITOR-ONLY BUG (cosmetic, front end was fine) — fixed `edit.js` in 18 more blocks:** `app-badges`, `cards`, `feature-stat`, `field-photos`, `hardware-lineup`, `hero`, `hero-blog`, `photo-grid`, `product-grid`, `segment-hero`, `testimonial-single`, `testimonials-carousel`, `two-column`, `two-column-alternating`, `two-column-animated`, `two-column-overlay`, `un-goals-nav`, `un-goals-two-column` — plus the background/device/phone images in the three hero-curved blocks (bundled into the same pass since those files were already open). Each now resolves via `useSelect( select => select('core').getEntityRecord('root','media', id) )`, falling back to the stored URL while the lookup is in flight or if the attachment was deleted. One additional gap caught during review: `pre-footer-cta`'s background image was still using the raw URL in `edit.js` despite `render.php` being correct — fixed the same way.

**Pattern cleanup:** found ~88 hardcoded fallback URLs pointing at old dev/staging domains (`ec2-100-25-145-190.compute-1.amazonaws.com`, `cropx-2026-2.local`) baked directly into the starter block-pattern templates (`patterns/page-*.php`, `404.php` — these seed a new page when an editor inserts one from the pattern library). Repointed all 88 at `https://new.cropx.com` (WordPress preserves `/wp-content/uploads/...` paths across domain moves, so this is a straight domain swap, not a guess) so a freshly-inserted pattern doesn't show broken images even before its ID-based resolution kicks in.

**Verification:** every touched block was diffed against a pre-edit backup and read through by hand; the full theme was built twice — once from the fixed source (forward) and once from the reconstructed pre-fix source (backup) — both compiled cleanly with webpack, and all 28 build outputs were confirmed to match. No PHP linter available in this environment, so PHP changes were verified by manual diff review rather than `php -l`.

**Files changed:** `functions.php` (`CROPX_THEME_VERSION` 0.4.48 → 0.4.49), `src/blocks/{28 blocks}/{edit.js and/or render.php}`, `patterns/page-*.php` (9 files), `404.php`, plus all 28 blocks' compiled `build/blocks/*` output.

**Delivered two zips**: `sitewide-media-fix-patch.zip` (forward — `functions.php`, `404.php`, `patterns/`, and `build/blocks/` for all 28 affected blocks) and `sitewide-media-fix-revert-backup.zip` (byte-verified pre-fix backup, same file set). Deploy: upload the patch zip into `wp-content/themes/cropx/` via WP File Manager and extract — same convention as every prior patch.

## September 9, 2026 — Logo Carousel: fixed broken logo images in the block editor

Lauren asked why so many images on new.cropx.com looked broken. Investigation (browser-checked the live homepage, a Results & Research story, and the Resources archive) found the front end was actually fine — every image resolved and loaded, zero real 404s. What she'd seen was a screenshot of the WordPress block editor, where the Logo Carousel ("TRUSTED BY LEADING BRANDS") showed broken-image icons for every logo.

**Root cause:** the Logo Carousel (`cropx/logo-strip`) stores each uploaded logo as `{ id, url, alt }`. `render.php` is defensive about this — it re-resolves the URL fresh from the attachment ID on every page load via `wp_get_attachment_url( $id )`, so the front end always shows the current, correct file location. `edit.js` was not — it used the raw `url` string saved at upload time, verbatim, forever. Since these logos were uploaded before the site moved onto the `new.cropx.com` domain, that saved URL pointed at a now-unreachable address, so only the editor canvas (and the sidebar's per-logo thumbnails) showed broken icons, while the live site was unaffected.

**Fix:** `edit.js` now resolves each logo's current URL the same way `render.php` does, via `useSelect( select => select('core').getEntityRecord('root','media', id) )` (the established pattern already used in `resource-downloads/edit.js` for the same kind of ID→URL lookup). Falls back to the stored `url` if the lookup is still in flight or the attachment was deleted, so this can only ever improve on the old behavior, never regress it. This also makes the block resilient to any future domain change — it won't go stale again.

**Files changed:** `functions.php` (`CROPX_THEME_VERSION` 0.4.47 → 0.4.48), `src/blocks/logo-strip/edit.js`, plus the compiled `build/blocks/logo-strip/*` output.

**Delivered two zips**: `logo-carousel-editor-fix-patch.zip` (forward patch — `functions.php` + `build/blocks/logo-strip/`) and `logo-carousel-editor-fix-revert-backup.zip` (byte-verified pre-fix backup). Deploy: upload the patch zip into `wp-content/themes/cropx/` via WP File Manager and extract — same small-patch convention as every prior fix in this file. This is editor-only cosmetic — no front-end deploy urgency, but worth pushing so future logo edits in wp-admin don't look broken.

## September 8, 2026 — Logo Carousel: scroll speed control

Lauren asked for a few slower speed options in the editor for the "Logo Carousel" block (`cropx/logo-strip`). Added a `speed` attribute (string enum: `normal` / `slow` / `slower`, default `normal`) alongside the existing `autoAdvance` toggle.

**How it works:** the marquee's `animation-duration` was hardcoded at `35s` in `style.css`. Swapped it for `var(--ls-duration, 35s)`, same pattern already used for `--ls-gap` (logo spacing). `render.php` and `edit.js` each carry a small `speed → seconds` lookup table (`normal: 35, slow: 55, slower: 80` — higher seconds = slower scroll, since it's the same distance spread over more time) and set `--ls-duration` inline. The two lookup tables are duplicated rather than shared, matching how `bgColor` validation is already duplicated between PHP and JS elsewhere in this block.

**Editor UX:** the new "Scroll speed" `SelectControl` only shows when Auto-advance is on (matches the existing pattern where speed is meaningless if the marquee isn't moving).

**Files changed:** `functions.php` (`CROPX_THEME_VERSION` 0.4.46 → 0.4.47), `src/blocks/logo-strip/block.json`, `style.css`, `render.php`, `edit.js`, plus the compiled `build/blocks/logo-strip/*` output.

**Delivered two zips** via the standard small-patch convention: `logo-carousel-speed-patch.zip` (forward patch — `functions.php` + `build/blocks/logo-strip/`) and `logo-carousel-speed-revert-backup.zip` (byte-verified pre-edit backup of the same files, for instant rollback if needed). Deploy: upload `logo-carousel-speed-patch.zip` into `wp-content/themes/cropx/` via WP File Manager and extract — it will place `functions.php` at the theme root and `build/blocks/logo-strip/*` in place, overwriting just those files.

## September 8, 2026 — PageSpeed regression investigation after render-blocking patch (task #617)

After the render-blocking async-CSS patch (see follow-up #2 below) went live, Lauren reported a sharp regression on both PageSpeed Insights (Desktop 89→54, Mobile 68→65, red/orange metrics) and lighthouse-metrics.com (28-54/100 across 6 global regions, "page loaded too slowly" warning) — asked me to independently verify.

**Ruled out a broken deploy:** direct browser testing of the live staging URL (unthrottled, real browser) showed fast TTFB, clean resource-timing waterfall, no console errors, no visual glitches. The site itself renders correctly and quickly under normal conditions — whatever is causing the bad Lighthouse-style scores, it isn't a broken/partial deploy.

**Confirmed the regression is real, not test noise:** the lighthouse-metrics.com report (6 geographically diverse regions, all catastrophic 28-54/100) independently corroborates Lauren's PSI screenshots. Two different tools, same conclusion.

**Working theory (unconfirmed either way):** CPU-credit exhaustion on the staging EC2 instance (t2/t3-family burstable instances throttle hard once their credit balance is spent — inflates TBT/LCP under heavy simulated-load testing like Lighthouse while leaving simple page loads fast, which matches what we're seeing). Repeated heavy Lighthouse-style test runs (mine and Lauren's, back to back) would be exactly the kind of load that burns through a credit pool. I could not confirm or rule this out — see below.

**Spent a large amount of effort trying to independently re-run Lighthouse and got blocked at every turn — this was a tooling problem on my end, not a data problem:**
- PSI's own public API (`pagespeedonline.googleapis.com`): returns `429 RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED` — zero daily quota for unauthenticated/no-API-key calls. Dead end without a Google Cloud API key.
- PSI's web UI (`pagespeed.web.dev`): hit a **reproducible frontend bug**, seen across 4 separate tabs/attempts. Sometimes the score cards get stuck showing "loading" placeholders forever even though the backend Lighthouse run completed (confirmed by reading the raw completed Lighthouse JSON straight out of the underlying network response — full `audits` object present, real data, just never rendered by the UI). In the most recent attempt, it was worse: the client made exactly one job-creation call and then never polled for the result at all — sat at "Running analysis / data loading" with zero further network activity for 4+ minutes. This looks like an environment-specific bug in this browser-automation tool, not a Google-side outage or a data problem — worth flagging if this comes up again in a future session so we don't re-burn time on it.
- Tried reading the raw completed Lighthouse JSON directly out of network responses to route around the broken UI — got audits data (confirmed real, matches the regression) but the `categories`/performance-score section sits *after* the (huge, hundreds-of-entries) `audits` object in the JSON, and every tool available for reading a captured network response body truncates at a fixed size well before reaching it. Tried fetch/XHR monkey-patching to intercept and extract just the score client-side — never fired, meaning the batchexecute calls aren't going through `window.fetch` or `XMLHttpRequest` in a way that's interceptable from the page's main JS realm (likely a Worker or similarly isolated context).
- lighthouse-metrics.com: hit its ~12-hour anonymous per-browser-fingerprint rate limit twice (once before a break, again immediately after) — waiting a short break did not reset it. Did not create an account to get around this, since account creation stays off-limits regardless of the reason.

**Net result: I was not able to get Lauren a fresh, independently-verified numeric score in this session.** What we do have: the regression is real and reproducible on external tools, the site itself isn't broken, and the only recent code change is the render-blocking async-CSS patch.

**Update: Lauren has already reverted** using the previously-prepared `render-blocking-revert-backup.zip` (uploaded via WP File Manager, same convention as every other patch in this file — overlays `inc/enqueue.php` and `functions.php` back to their pre-patch state, `functions.php` should show `CROPX_THEME_VERSION` back at `0.4.45`). Plan is to re-test PageSpeed/lighthouse-metrics.com tomorrow (Sept 9) once any CPU-credit throttling on the EC2 instance has had a full day to clear, to see whether scores recover on their own (reverted patch + rested instance) before deciding whether the render-blocking patch is safe to re-apply. **Next session: re-run PSI (both mobile + desktop) and lighthouse-metrics.com against the staging URL and compare against the pre-patch baseline (mobile ~68, desktop ~89) and the regression numbers (mobile 65, desktop 54, lighthouse-metrics 28-54 across regions) to see where things land now that both the patch is reverted and time has passed.**

## September 8, 2026 — PageSpeed follow-up #2: async-load below-the-fold CSS (task #616)

Continuing the render-blocking-requests item deferred from the previous entry. Re-ran PageSpeed after the image-dims/minification patch landed: mobile Performance 68 (LCP 7.4s, CLS 0.145), desktop 89 (LCP 1.9s, CLS 0.019) — minify-CSS savings dropped to a trivial 4 KiB on both, confirming the earlier minification patch is live and working.

Expanded PageSpeed's "Render-blocking requests" insight (1,020ms mobile / 420ms desktop) to see the actual list rather than guessing: 14 blocking requests, roughly 69 KiB ours vs 47 KiB third-party. Ours: the 3 sitewide globals (`tokens.css`/`shared.css`/`content.css`), `footer.css`, the theme's base `style.css` shim, plus whichever blocks happen to be on the homepage — `nav`, `segments`, `zoho-contact-form`, `two-column-animated` — each contributing their own auto-registered block stylesheet. Third-party: Complianz's cookie-banner CSS (`banner-1-optin.css`, `cookieblocker.min.css`) and TranslatePress's language-switcher CSS + JS. Confirmed via `inc/enqueue.php` that none of our own JS is the problem — every script we enqueue already uses `strategy => defer, in_footer => true` (why mobile TBT is 0ms) — this was purely a CSS-loading-order problem.

Not everything on that list is equally safe to touch, so this pass deliberately only fixes a subset:

**Async-loaded (this patch):** `footer.css`, `two-column-animated`'s block CSS, `zoho-contact-form`'s block CSS, and TranslatePress's `trp-language-switcher-v2.css` — all confirmed below-the-fold (or, for the TranslatePress switcher, a small nav sub-element whose styling can lag by a beat with zero visual risk). Added a `style_loader_tag` filter to `inc/enqueue.php` that rewrites these four to the standard "loadCSS" pattern: `media="print"` so the browser fetches at low priority without blocking first paint, then `onload` swaps `media` back to the original value once the file has actually arrived, with a `<noscript>` fallback for JS-disabled visitors. Matches by URL fragment (`strpos($href, ...)`) rather than enqueue handle, since two of the four targets are WordPress's own auto-generated block-style handles that aren't hardcoded anywhere in this file to match against — matching on the literal file path is more robust than trying to reverse-engineer WP core's handle-naming convention.

**Deliberately left alone:**
- The 3 sitewide globals (`tokens.css`/`shared.css`/`content.css`) — these carry base typography/resets/section utilities used above the fold on every template. Deferring them wholesale would cause a flash of unstyled content. The real fix here is a critical-CSS-inlining pass (inline just the above-the-fold subset in `<head>`, defer the rest) — bigger, separate piece of work, not done in this patch.
- `nav.css` and `segments.css` — both style above-the-fold content (nav is always visible; segments is the hero-equivalent on templates that use it), same FOUC risk.
- Complianz's cookie-banner CSS — deliberately NOT deferred. The banner needs to be capable of rendering before other scripts run to satisfy consent-before-tracking requirements; loosening that timing is a compliance question, not just a performance one. Worth checking whether Complianz has its own built-in "defer assets" setting before touching this with a blanket theme-level filter.
- TranslatePress's `trp-frontend-language-switcher.js` (the JS, not the CSS) — script-loading changes need a separate `script_loader_tag` filter and weren't in scope for this pass; flagged as a possible next step, not done here.

`CROPX_THEME_VERSION` bumped `0.4.45` → `0.4.46` (no CSS/JS file *content* changed here, just how they're loaded — the bump isn't strictly required for cache-busting purposes the way CLAUDE.md gotcha #10 describes, but kept for consistency with every other deploy in this file). Delivered as `render-blocking-patch.zip` (2 files: `inc/enqueue.php`, `functions.php` — no `build/` sync needed since `inc/` files load directly from theme root, unlike block `render.php`) with `render-blocking-revert-backup.zip` as the pre-edit pair.

**Staging deploy steps:** upload `render-blocking-patch.zip` into `wp-content/themes/cropx/` and extract there — overlays `inc/enqueue.php` and `functions.php` in place. To verify it took: view source on the homepage and confirm the footer/two-column-animated/zoho-contact-form/TranslatePress stylesheet `<link>` tags now show `media="print" onload="this.media='all'..."` instead of `media="all"` directly. To roll back, upload+extract `render-blocking-revert-backup.zip` the same way.

**Still open from the render-blocking investigation:** the critical-CSS-inlining pass for the 3 global files (biggest remaining chunk of "ours" weight), and whether to defer TranslatePress's JS via `script_loader_tag`. Both deliberately deferred pending Lauren's go-ahead — same "worry about it after retesting" pattern as the rest of this PageSpeed work.

## September 8, 2026 — PageSpeed follow-up: image width/height sitewide + global CSS minification (tasks #611-615)

Continuing the PageSpeed audit from task #465 ("worry about some of the other items after re-testing" — Lauren's words), fixed the two remaining safe/fast items: the "Image elements do not have explicit width and height" diagnostic (contributes to the 0.141 CLS score) and "Minify CSS" (57 KiB across the 3 sitewide global stylesheets). Render-blocking-request reorganization and the "9 non-composited animations" audit are still deliberately deferred pending re-testing, per Lauren's own instruction.

**CSS minification:** `styles/tokens.css`, `shared.css`, `content.css` — the three global, unminified stylesheets enqueued sitewide via `enqueue_block_assets` in `inc/enqueue.php` — run through `clean-css` at level-1 (whitespace/comment stripping + known-safe shorthand rewrites, e.g. `background: transparent` → `background: 0 0`). Verified semantically lossless with a `tinycss2`-based comparison script (parses both versions into selector/property/value tuples, treats comma-separated selector lists as an unordered set, normalizes whitespace/number formatting) rather than trusting the minifier blindly — the one flagged "risky-looking" transform (`transparent` → `0 0` on `.pg-block`) was manually confirmed to be computed-style-identical, not a bug. Sizes: `tokens.css` 24,081 → 4,982 bytes, `shared.css` 29,868 → 7,948 bytes, `content.css` 27,869 → 12,462 bytes (~56.5 KiB saved). Same filenames/enqueue paths, so no PHP changes needed for this part.

**Image width/height:** added a new shared helper, `cropx_img_dims_attr( int $attachment_id = 0, string $fallback_url = '' ): string` (end of `inc/helpers.php`) — resolves an attachment ID (or, failing that, a URL via `attachment_url_to_postid()`) to a ready-to-echo `' width="X" height="Y"'` string via `wp_get_attachment_metadata()`, returning `''` on any failure so it degrades gracefully. Almost every block that builds `<img>` markup by hand already had a `wp_get_attachment_image()` branch when an attachment ID was tracked (that WP core function adds width/height automatically) — the actual gaps were always in the `elseif`/`else` fallback branches (URL-only, no ID) or in blocks that never tracked an ID at all (product-sections, product-tabs, logo-strip).

Patched with `cropx_img_dims_attr()`: `hero`, `hero-curved`, `hero-curved-standard`, `hero-curved-animated`, `hero-blog`, `segment-hero` (bg image, all 6 hero-family LCP images — plus their `device`/`phone` illustration fallback `<img>`s, found on a second, more careful sweep after the first pass only covered the primary bg image), `cards`, `feature-stat`, `field-photos`, `photo-grid`, `product-grid` (×3), `product-sections` (×3, no ID tracked → `0` literal), `product-tabs` (×3, same), `testimonial-single`, `two-column-alternating`, `two-column`, `un-goals-nav`, `un-goals-two-column` (×2), `people-showcase`, `app-badges` (×2), `logo-strip` (×4, no ID tracked), `two-column-video`, `video`. Static theme assets got hardcoded intrinsic dimensions instead (no attachment ID exists for these): `footer`/`footer-brand`'s wordmark SVG (`width="111" height="34"`, rounded from its `0 0 110.732 33.51949` viewBox), and the 4 curved-hero blocks' default illustration fallbacks (`vertex-partial-a.png` 465×1400, `phone-mockup-b.png` 800×998 — read via PIL).

**Bugs caught and fixed before they reached Lauren:** (1) an `<img\b[^>]*>` regex scanner used to find gaps kept false-triggering on embedded `?>` PHP closing tags inside multi-line `<img>` blocks — fixed by substituting `?>` with a sentinel before matching; a second sweep also had to exclude docblock/comment lines (`// <img fetchpriority...` in a code comment was flagged as a "gap"). (2) The first attempt at inserting `cropx_img_dims_attr()` into the hero-family `device`/`phone` fallback strings placed the call *inside* the `alt="..."` attribute's quotes instead of after them (e.g. `alt="...soil sensor width="465" height="1400"" loading=...` — malformed HTML) — caught by manual inspection, not by the automated checks, so worth double-checking any future hand-built-string `<img>` edit renders correctly rather than just checking it doesn't error.

**Verification (no PHP interpreter in the sandbox):** re-ran the corrected scanner across all `src/blocks/*/render.php` and confirmed zero remaining width/height gaps sitewide. Used `grep`-based paren/quote-balance checks on every edited line as a lightweight syntax-safety proxy (same method used successfully for the earlier lazy-loading patch).

**Deploy structure reminder for future patches:** blocks are registered from `build/blocks/<name>/` (see `inc/blocks.php`), not `src/blocks/<name>/` — `render.php`/`block.json` aren't webpack-transformed, so the build step just copies them verbatim, but that means **any `src/blocks/*/render.php` edit must also be copied into the matching `build/blocks/*/render.php`** before packaging a deploy zip, or the change won't reach the live site even though the repo/local-install source looks right. Confirmed `src`/`build` were byte-identical pre-edit for an untouched block, then copied all 25 edited `render.php` files from `src/` to `build/` to keep them in sync — this patch's zip targets `build/blocks/<name>/render.php`, matching every prior patch in this file.

`CROPX_THEME_VERSION` bumped `0.4.44` → `0.4.45`. Delivered as `img-dims-patch.zip` (30 files: `inc/helpers.php`, `functions.php`, 3 minified `styles/*.css`, 25 `build/blocks/*/render.php`) with `img-dims-revert-backup.zip` as the pre-edit byte-for-byte revert pair, per Lauren's standing "make it easy to revert" requirement.

**Staging deploy steps (WP File Manager, per CLAUDE.md's small-patch convention):** upload `img-dims-patch.zip` into `wp-content/themes/cropx/` and extract there — it overlays `functions.php`, `inc/helpers.php`, the 3 `styles/*.css` files, and the 25 `build/blocks/<name>/render.php` files in place (no new folders, nothing to collide with). If a rollback is ever needed, upload+extract `img-dims-revert-backup.zip` the same way, then re-check `functions.php` shows `0.4.44` again.

## August 24, 2026 — Footer rebuild, phase 1: Synced Pattern + 2 new small blocks (task #305)

Lauren asked to start rebuilding the footer using native WordPress capabilities instead of the old monolithic `cropx/footer` block (~15 hardcoded attributes controlling column headings, brand content, and 5 social URLs — any structural change needed a code edit). Per her explicit instruction, all research was based on the **live staging site**, not the local WP install (stale) or old doc comments in the repo (one was actually out of date — see below).

**What staging already had right:** every footer nav column (Hardware, Software, Solutions, Knowledge Hub, About, Contact, plus the legal bar) already pulls live from real WordPress Nav Menus via `wp_nav_menu()` — the exact same menus that power the header dropdowns (`inc/menus.php`'s `CropX_Footer_Walker` + `cropx_footer_platform_children()` helper for the Platform Mega Menu's Hardware/Software sub-groups). Link data was never the problem; only the *structure* around it was monolithic.

**Architecture (confirmed with Lauren via a 3-way choice — she picked this over full Site Editor/Template Part support, which this classic PHP theme doesn't support without a much bigger FSE lift):**

1. **A Synced Pattern** (`Appearance → Patterns`, slug `footer`) built entirely from native core blocks — Group (for structure/layout), Heading (column titles), Paragraph (copyright line) — so Lauren can reorder columns, retitle headings, and rearrange the brand block directly in the block editor, no code required.
2. **`cropx/menu-links`** (new, small) — one attribute (`source`, a dropdown of the 7 real sources: Hardware, Software, Solutions, Knowledge Hub, About, Contact, Legal). Renders raw `<a>` tags via the existing `CropX_Footer_Walker`/`cropx_footer_platform_children()` — zero new PHP logic, just exposes the existing menu system as a droppable block. No wrapper markup at all on the front end, so it drops straight into a `.footer-nav-group` Group block and all the existing CSS keeps matching unchanged. Editor preview uses `ServerSideRender` (the standard WP component for "preview real PHP output live") rather than a hand-built JS mirror, since re-implementing the walker logic in JS would just be duplicated logic that could drift.
3. **`cropx/footer-brand`** (new, small) — logo, tagline, contact rows, social icons. Kept as one block rather than decomposed into core Image/Paragraph/Social Links blocks because the contact/social icons are inline `currentColor` SVGs whose color must track link hover state — core blocks can't do that without a second hover-state image asset per icon.

**CSS relocation (important structural decision):** the footer's CSS (`.footer`, `.ftr-c`, `.footer-main`, `.footer-nav-columns`, `.footer-legal`, etc.) used to auto-load only because the old monolithic block's `block.json` declared it as that block's own `style`. Now that the footer is split across a pattern + 2 blocks, no single block "owns" that shared structural CSS. Moved it verbatim to a new sitewide `styles/footer.css`, enqueued unconditionally in `inc/enqueue.php` (footer is on every page) via the existing `enqueue_block_assets` hook — this also means it loads correctly while editing the pattern itself in `Appearance → Patterns`, not just on the front end.

**One deliberately-kept dynamic piece:** the copyright line's year. Added a tiny `[cropx_year]` shortcode (`inc/helpers.php`) so Lauren never has to manually bump it each January. Gotcha worth remembering: shortcodes only auto-expand inside `the_content()` — block content rendered via `do_blocks()` outside the main loop (which is exactly how the footer pattern gets rendered) does **not** run shortcodes automatically. `footer.php` now wraps the call in `do_shortcode( do_blocks( ... ) )` to handle this — any future shortcode typed into any block inside the footer pattern will work correctly because of this wrapper.

**`footer.php`** now looks up the pattern by slug via the existing `cropx_get_synced_block_ref( 'footer' )` idiom (same as `segment-navigation` and the per-page pre-footer CTA patterns) and falls back to the old `cropx/footer` block if the pattern hasn't been created yet on an environment — so nothing breaks mid-rollout.

**Correction (Aug 24, 2026, same day):** the `footer-synced-pattern.html` markup delivered initially used `"layout":{"type":"flow"}` on every Group block, which threw "This block has encountered an error and cannot be previewed" in the editor — `"flow"` isn't a registered layout type. The correct value for plain flow-behavior layout is `"default"` (confusingly, "flow" is the *name* of the behavior, but `"default"` is the accepted JSON value — verified against WordPress core docs). Fixed in the delivered file; re-paste it into the pattern's Code Editor if you hit that error.

**Correction #2 (Aug 24, 2026):** once the pattern was live, Lauren reported the front-end footer looked correct but the block editor's canvas preview showed each nav column's links all run together on one line, no breaks. Root cause: `cropx/menu-links`'s front-end `render.php` outputs zero wrapper markup around its `<a>` tags (by design — see above), which is exactly what lets `.footer-nav-group`'s `display:flex; flex-direction:column` stack them correctly in the DOM. In the editor, the block still needs its own selectable wrapper div, and `ServerSideRender` renders the live PHP output inside that div — which takes the `<a>` tags out of the `.footer-nav-group` flex context, so they fell back to normal inline flow. Fixed in `src/blocks/menu-links/editor.css` with a plain `.menu-links-editor-wrap a { display: block; margin-bottom: 0.75rem }` descendant rule — editor-only, doesn't touch the (already-correct) front-end output. `CROPX_THEME_VERSION` bumped to `0.2.3`. Delivered as a small follow-up patch, `menu-links-editor-fix.zip` — just the `menu-links` block folder + `functions.php`.

**Follow-up polish (Aug 24, 2026):** Lauren asked for two visual tweaks once the pattern was live —

1. The tagline and locations line were wrapping unevenly (a long first line + a short orphaned word on the second). Added `text-wrap: balance` to `.footer-tagline`, and wrapped the locations text in a new `.footer-locations-text` span (inside the existing pin-icon `<a>`, in both `footer-brand/render.php` and `edit.js`) with the same rule — `text-wrap: balance` is a standard, well-supported progressive enhancement that picks better line-break points; unsupported browsers just fall back to normal wrapping, so there's no risk.
2. The horizontal rule above the copyright/legal links wasn't reliably spanning the full viewport width. Since the footer's own width now depends on whatever wraps it (Synced Pattern content, not one block anymore), added the standard "full-bleed" CSS to `.footer-legal` — `width: 100vw` pulled back to the viewport edges with negative margins — so it's no longer dependent on any assumption about ancestor containers. This is safe from the horizontal-scroll bug documented in `shared.css` (`overflow-x: clip` on `html`/`body` silently absorbs any sub-pixel rounding from the 100vw math instead of creating a visible scrollbar).

`CROPX_THEME_VERSION` bumped to `0.2.4`. Delivered as `footer-polish-fix.zip` — just the `footer-brand` block folder + `styles/footer.css` + `functions.php`.

**Follow-up #2 (Aug 24, 2026): white gap above the footer's border-top on "many pages if not all."** Lauren spotted a white gap between the bottom of the last content section and the footer's top border. Confirmed the root cause directly on the homepage via browser inspection rather than guessing: an empty trailing `<p class="wp-block-paragraph"></p>` — an easy, invisible-in-the-editor accident — sits as the very last element in the page content, right after the Pre-footer CTA block. It has a 16px `margin-bottom`, and none of its ancestors (`article`, `main.site-content`, `.site`) have any border/padding/overflow of their own, so per normal CSS margin-collapsing rules that 16px "collapses through" all of them and lands as invisible whitespace between the last visible section and the footer, rather than being contained where it visually belongs. This is a generic WordPress footgun — any page whose content happens to end in an empty paragraph will show the identical gap — which matches Lauren seeing it on multiple pages rather than just one.

Fixed with a permanent, sitewide CSS backstop rather than hunting down every stray empty paragraph individually: `.site-content { padding-bottom: 1px; }` in `styles/shared.css`, right next to the existing horizontal-overflow safety net (same defensive philosophy — a small structural rule that protects every current and future page from this entire class of bug, 1px is not visually perceptible, and unlike `overflow: hidden` it can't clip any of the several blocks that intentionally bleed decorative elements past their own box). Note this is a backstop, not a content cleanup — if Lauren wants the actual stray empty paragraph(s) gone from page content, that still needs manual removal in the editor (worth checking whether it's baked into a reused Pre-footer CTA Synced Pattern, in which case removing it once there would clean up every page that uses that pattern).

`CROPX_THEME_VERSION` bumped to `0.2.5`. Delivered as `footer-gap-fix.zip` — just `styles/shared.css` + `functions.php`.

**Correction #3 (Aug 24, 2026):** Lauren reported the gap was still visible on the homepage after the `.site-content { padding-bottom: 1px }` fix above shipped. Re-measured live and confirmed the fix was deployed correctly (`shared.css?ver=0.2.5` was loading) but hadn't actually closed the visible gap — the padding-bottom trick stops a margin from *collapsing through* a container, but it doesn't remove the margin. The empty paragraph's 16px was still rendering as real, visible space; the fix only changed which element the browser structurally credits that space to (now correctly inside `.site-content` instead of leaking past it), which fixes margin-collapse side effects but not what's on screen. Re-measured directly: the gap between the last visible section and the footer's border-top was unchanged at ~16-17px even with the fix live.

Actual fix: `p.wp-block-paragraph:empty { display: none; }` — an empty paragraph block has no content to lose, so hiding it entirely removes both its box and its margin, which is what actually closes the visible gap. Kept the `.site-content` padding-bottom rule too (still useful as a backstop against margin-collapse side effects from *real* trailing content, just not what fixes this specific bug). `CROPX_THEME_VERSION` bumped to `0.2.6`. Delivered as `footer-gap-fix-v2.zip` — `styles/shared.css` + `functions.php`.

**Not yet done / next steps:**
- The Synced Pattern itself must be created **directly in wp-admin** on staging (Appearance → Patterns → Add New → set slug to exactly `footer` → switch to Code Editor view in the block toolbar's "..." menu → paste the markup from `footer-synced-pattern.html`, delivered alongside this patch → make sure "Synced" is enabled, not "Standalone"). Patterns are database content, not theme files — code alone can't create them, same caveat already documented for `segment-navigation`.
- Old `cropx/footer` block folder (`src/blocks/footer/`) is untouched and still fully functional as the fallback — haven't discussed with Lauren whether/when to retire it. Its CSS is now duplicated in `styles/footer.css`, which is harmless (identical rules, just loaded twice temporarily) but worth cleaning up once the new pattern is confirmed live and stable.
- `CROPX_THEME_VERSION` bumped to `0.2.2` (functions.php) per the cache-busting convention — needed since block CSS/JS changed.

**Delivered:** `footer-rebuild-patch.zip` (small — 4 changed root-level files + 2 brand-new block folders, not a full theme re-upload) plus `footer-synced-pattern.html` (the pattern markup to paste in Code Editor view). See deploy steps below.

**Staging deploy steps (WP File Manager, per CLAUDE.md's small-patch convention):**
1. Upload `footer-rebuild-patch.zip` into `wp-content/themes/cropx/` and extract there — it overlays `functions.php`, `footer.php`, `inc/helpers.php`, `inc/enqueue.php`, `styles/footer.css` in place, and adds two brand-new folders (`build/blocks/menu-links/`, `build/blocks/footer-brand/`) that can't collide with anything existing.
2. In wp-admin, go to Appearance → Patterns → Add New. Set the pattern's slug to exactly `footer` (Settings sidebar → the pattern's own slug field, not the title — title can be anything like "Footer"). Switch to the block toolbar's "..." menu → Code Editor, paste the contents of `footer-synced-pattern.html`, switch back to Visual editor to confirm it renders, and make sure "Synced" is toggled on (not "Not synced").
3. Reload the front end of any page — the footer should now render from the new pattern. If it still shows the old layout, double-check the pattern's slug is exactly `footer` (case-sensitive, no typos) — `footer.php` falls back silently to the old block if the slug lookup fails, so a typo won't error, it'll just look unchanged.
4. Spot-check: resize to mobile width (the 900px/540px responsive breakpoints should still stack/reflow exactly as before), and confirm all 6 nav columns' links still match what's in Appearance → Menus.

## August 24, 2026 — New block: App Store Badges (cropx/app-badges)

Lauren asked for a very simple new block: a centered row of "Download on the App Store" / "Get it on Google Play" badges, matching the old site's version but not needing to match it exactly. Requirements: top/bottom margins matching Logo Carousel, the same 3 background options used everywhere else (White / Taupe 50 / Deep Blue + Topo, with yesterday's drift-pattern.svg base64-bloat gotcha in mind), and an optional eyebrow using the same styling options as Logo Carousel's.

**Recommendation on eyebrow text:** left off by default. The old site's version had no label above the badges and reads fine that way — the badges are self-explanatory. Built the toggle in anyway (off by default) with the same eyebrow text + color options as Logo Carousel (CropX Blue / Deep Blue / White), in case Lauren wants to add something like "Get the CropX app" later.

**Built `src/blocks/app-badges/`** (block.json, index.js, edit.js, render.php, style.css, editor.css) — new block, category `cropx-cta`:
- Section padding matches Logo Carousel's `.logo-strip` exactly (`padding: 3rem 0`).
- `bgColor` enum `taupe` (default) / `white` / `deep-blue`, with the deep-blue variant getting the drift-pattern topo overlay via the same `--ab-pattern-url` CSS-custom-property injection technique as every other block that's been through the Aug 2026 PageSpeed fix (real, cacheable URL from `render.php`/`edit.js`, never a relative `url()` in `style.css` — that's what base64-inlines a ~90KB SVG and has previously broken WP File Manager zip deploys on other blocks).
- Two independent badge slots (App Store, Google Play), each with its own Media Library upload field, link URL, and alt text — no reorder UI, since the scope is exactly these two fixed badges.

**Badge artwork is intentionally NOT bundled with the theme or reproduced by me.** Apple's and Google's official badges are trademarked marketing assets, each with their own usage guidelines, published by Apple and Google directly through their own badge-generator pages. Lauren (or whoever manages the app listings) should download the *official* artwork from those pages and upload it through the block's sidebar — that keeps the theme itself trademark-clean and guarantees the badge always matches whatever the current official version looks like. Until an image is uploaded, that badge simply isn't rendered on the front end (same graceful-degrade behavior as Logo Carousel with zero logos) — the editor canvas shows a small dashed-outline "+ Upload badge" placeholder instead of a broken image.

Built and rsynced to local WordPress (task #511).

**Follow-up:** Lauren asked for a left/center/right alignment option for the badge row. Added an `alignment` attribute (enum `left`/`center`/`right`, default `center`) — a "Badge alignment" `SelectControl` in Section Settings, `.ab-row--left`/`.ab-row--right` CSS modifiers (default/center needs no modifier class), and the same class applied to both the front-end render and the editor canvas so they stay in sync.

**Delivered for staging:** `app-badges-block-patch.zip` — a real `npm run build` was run (this block has actual JSX in `edit.js`, so unlike the PHP-only patches above it can't be hand-packaged), confirmed the compiled CSS has no base64-bloated SVG, and zipped just `build/blocks/app-badges/`. Since this is a brand-new block folder rather than an overwrite of existing files, it's not exposed to the large-zip mixed-generation extraction bug — upload directly into `wp-content/themes/cropx/build/blocks/` and extract.

## August 24, 2026 — Sitewide: PDF links now open in browser instead of force-downloading

Lauren flagged that clicking a PDF link anywhere on the site dropped the file straight into the visitor's downloads folder, and asked for a recommendation: also force a download on top of opening in-browser, or open-in-browser only?

**Recommendation (and what got implemented): open in browser only, no forced download.** Every modern browser's built-in PDF viewer already has its own visible Download button, so a visitor who wants a local copy can still get one in one extra click — but a visitor who just wants to *look at* the brochure isn't stuck digging through their downloads folder or getting a surprise file dump. Force-downloading on top of opening in a tab would just be redundant and mildly startling. `single-cropx_publication.php`'s existing Download CTA already worked this way (`target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"`, no `download` attribute) — this change brings the rest of the site in line with that existing, correct pattern.

**Every anchor that previously carried a `download` attribute (static or conditional via `$x_is_file`) was converted to `target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"` instead**, across 7 files:
- `src/blocks/resource-downloads/render.php` — all 3 branches (version-picker button, single-version button, legacy-URL fallback button).
- `single-cropx_resource.php` — same 3 branches, since it mirrors that block's markup directly.
- `src/blocks/pre-footer-cta/render.php`, `src/blocks/mid-page-cta/render.php`, `src/blocks/hero-curved/render.php`, `src/blocks/hero-curved-standard/render.php`, `src/blocks/hero-curved-animated/render.php` — each of these 5 blocks' primary/secondary CTA can point at a media-library file (added in the "media-file-download CTA" feature); the conditional `<?php echo $x_is_file ? ' download' : ''; ?>` pattern in each became `<?php echo $x_is_file ? ' target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"' : ''; ?>`.

No JS changes needed — `resource-downloads/view.js` only ever touches the `href` attribute on version-select, never `download`.

**Delivered:** `pdf-open-in-browser-patch.zip` — targets `wp-content/themes/cropx/build/blocks/{resource-downloads,pre-footer-cta,mid-page-cta,hero-curved,hero-curved-standard,hero-curved-animated}/render.php` plus `wp-content/themes/cropx/single-cropx_resource.php`. These are PHP-only `render.php` files referenced directly by each block's `block.json` (`"render": "file:./render.php"`) — webpack never touches them, so no `npm run build` step is needed; the patch zip's paths already match the deployed `build/blocks/...` location directly. Verified via the same manual brace/paren/bracket balance script as prior rounds (all 7 files balanced) since PHP CLI still isn't available in this environment.

## August 24, 2026 — Simple CPT templates, round 4: fixed inactive dealers still showing on /dealer-archive/

Lauren reported inactive dealers (`dealer_active` = '0') still appearing on the Dealers archive, despite round-1 already including a `pre_get_posts` filter meant to exclude them. Root cause was an actual bug, not a missing feature — two things wrong with the original filter:

1. **It was registered inside `archive-cropx_dealer.php` itself** — a template file. Template files are only `include`d by `template-loader.php` *after* WordPress has already built and executed the main query. A `pre_get_posts` callback added at that point can never affect the very page's own listing it was meant to filter — it was structurally too late, every single time.
2. It checked the global `is_post_type_archive( 'cropx_dealer' )` function rather than `$query->is_post_type_archive( 'cropx_dealer' )` — the global conditional tags aren't reliably populated yet this early in the main query lifecycle. `inc/insights-archive.php`'s near-identical category-widening filter already uses the `$query->` method form for this exact reason — the dealer filter just hadn't matched that established convention.

**Fix:** moved the filter into `inc/cpts.php` (loaded at theme bootstrap, well before any query runs), right next to the `dealer_active` meta registration, using `$query->is_main_query() && $query->is_post_type_archive( 'cropx_dealer' )`. Removed the non-functional copy from `archive-cropx_dealer.php` and updated its doc comment to point at the real implementation and explain why it can't live in the template file.

**Delivered:** updated `inc/cpts.php` and `archive-cropx_dealer.php` — small enough to hand off as just these 2 files rather than a full re-zip of all 11.

## August 24, 2026 — Simple CPT templates, round 3: landscape document covers on the Resources archive

Lauren sent a screenshot showing exactly the problem: a landscape brochure ("CropX System – Detailed Overview") was getting its left/right edges sliced off by the archive card's fixed-portrait `object-fit:cover` frame — the round-2 fix only handled the plain crop-from-bottom case, not landscape source documents.

Rather than reimplement the fix, the archive card's cover markup now calls the exact same cover-resolution + landscape-detection logic as `resource-downloads/render.php` (attachment ID from `download_attachment_id` → featured image → skip, then `width > height` on the resolved `cropx-doc-cover` image size) and renders the identical two-layer treatment when landscape: a full-bleed blurred `.rsd-cover--bg` copy behind, and the untouched page on top sized to fit by width (`.rsd-cover--fg`, `object-fit: contain`). This reuses that block's own CSS classes directly — no parallel copy — so the two places can never visually drift apart. Portrait documents (the majority) are unchanged: single image, plain `object-fit: cover`.

This also simplified the card markup: the earlier `.scpt-card-img--doc` modifier (round 2's partial fix) is gone entirely — the archive card's cover now renders `.rsd-cover-wrap`/`.rsd-cover` directly instead of wrapping it in a `.scpt-card-img` div, since the block's own wrapper already does everything needed (aspect-ratio, background, overflow clipping). Extended the existing `is_singular('cropx_resource')` enqueue condition in `inc/enqueue.php` to also fire on `is_post_type_archive('cropx_resource')`, so the archive gets the same `resource-downloads` block CSS the single page already needed.

**Delivered:** `simple-cpt-templates-deploy-v3.zip` (same 11 files as v2 — supersedes it, upload this one). Still no `npm run build` needed. Verified via the same manual brace/paren/bracket balance check (PHP CLI still unavailable in this environment).

## August 24, 2026 — Simple CPT templates, round 2: URL slug rework + resource download buttons

Follow-up feedback on the 6 new templates from the entry directly below.

**Slug rework — all three CPTs moved off their "plain" URL, on purpose.** Lauren wants each CPT archive living at a dedicated `-archive`-suffixed slug rather than the plain top-level word, so that word stays free for a real WP Page later without ever risking the exact `has_archive` collision `cropx_dealer` already had a documented history of (see the entry below and CLAUDE.md). Confirmed the intended slug via a quick clarifying question (her message used "resource-archive" once and "resources-archive" twice) — she confirmed **`/resources-archive/`**.
- `cropx_resource`: rewrite slug `resources` → `resources-archive`.
- `cropx_team_member`: rewrite slug `team` → `team-archive` (this also fixes the 404 she hit clicking "Back to Team" — the archive template existed and `has_archive` was already `true`, but permalinks likely hadn't been re-saved after the first deploy; the slug change forces that anyway).
- `cropx_dealer`: rewrite slug `dealers` → `dealer-archive`. This one's different: `/dealers/` isn't just "the old slug for this CPT" — it's the URL of a *separate*, real WordPress Page (the interactive dealer-finder map, currently at `/solutions/dealers/`... or rather, Lauren wants `/dealers/` to redirect *there*, not to the new archive). So the redirect map is asymmetric on purpose:
  - `/resources/` → `/resources-archive/`
  - `/team/` → `/team-archive/`
  - `/dealers/` → `/solutions/dealers/` (NOT `/dealer-archive/`)

**New file `inc/legacy-redirects.php`** implements exactly those three 301s via a `template_redirect` hook guarded by `is_404()` (so it can never hijack a real, resolving page — only fires when nothing else claimed the URL). This is a narrow stopgap, not a replacement for the still-pending "Install + configure the Redirection plugin" item — it only covers these 3 specific paths. Required in `functions.php` with the same `file_exists()` guard as `inc/duplicate-post.php`, given this codebase's documented history of WP File Manager silently dropping files on large-zip extraction.

**IMPORTANT for Lauren after deploying:** Settings → Permalinks needs to be re-saved once on staging to flush all three new rewrite rules, or `/resources-archive/`, `/team-archive/`, and `/dealer-archive/` will all 404 until then (same gotcha already documented for `/story-tag/`).

**Resource archive cards — thumbnail framing only.** Per Lauren: "should look a bit more like the cards in the 3-wide version of the resource grid block, in terms of how the thumbnail image fits in its frame. That should be the only similarity though." Added a `.scpt-card-img--doc` modifier matching only `.rsd-cover-wrap`/`.rsd-cover`'s framing — fixed 8.5:11 (US Letter) aspect-ratio, `object-fit: cover` anchored top-center so any cropping comes off the bottom of the document, never the top. Everything else about the card (shape, radius, shadow, badge, buttons) is untouched and unrelated to that block.

**Resource single thumbnail — uniform 2px corners.** New `.scpt-detail-photo--doc` modifier overrides the default asymmetric `--radius-card` with a plain `border-radius: 2px` on all four corners, applied only on `single-cropx_resource.php` — Dealers/Team detail photos are unaffected.

**Delivered:** `simple-cpt-templates-deploy-v2.zip` (11 files: the 6 templates, `styles/simple-cpt.css`, `inc/cpts.php`, `inc/enqueue.php`, the new `inc/legacy-redirects.php`, and `functions.php`). Supersedes the round-1 zip below — upload this one instead. Still pure PHP/CSS, no `npm run build` needed (the resource-downloads block itself wasn't touched — its already-built `build/blocks/resource-downloads/` assets are just referenced, not changed). ## August 24, 2026 — Built 6 new "simple CPT" templates: Resources, Dealers, Team (archive + single each)

Lauren asked for basic archive/single page templates for three CPTs — Resources, Dealers, Team — modeled explicitly on `page.php` ("very simple, using the most basic pages we have — like the Terms & Conditions page — as a reference point"), no newsletter subscribe CTA on any of them, and each one using a not-yet-created "Fallback Cat. Pg. Demo Form + CTA" synced pattern in place of the plain default pre-footer CTA block — the same lookup-by-slug-with-fallback idiom used throughout the theme (`category.php`, `tag.php`, `home.php`).

**New files:**
- `archive-cropx_resource.php` / `single-cropx_resource.php` — plain card grid + detail page. `cropx_resource` already had `has_archive => true` and `rewrite['slug'] => 'resources'`, so no CPT registration change was needed here. Single page uses `cropx_resource_get_available_versions()` (the same helper the resource-downloads block uses) to render one direct-download button per available language/format — no JS, no `<select>`, just plain links, since a page this simple doesn't need the block's dropdown-and-live-cover machinery.
- `archive-cropx_dealer.php` (replacing the old empty placeholder) / `single-cropx_dealer.php` — plain list of dealers (name, region, address, phone) linking to a detail page (address/phone/email/website). The archive filters to `dealer_active != '0'` via a scoped `pre_get_posts` hook in the template itself, matching the dealer-finder map block's own filtering; the single template does NOT filter on active status — a directly-linked dealer page should still load even if that dealer's been hidden from the map/list.
- `archive-cropx_team_member.php` / `single-cropx_team_member.php` — plain headshot grid (name, job title) linking to a detail page (photo, job title, bio, optional LinkedIn link).

**Registration change required:** building the Dealers and Team *archive* pages necessarily required flipping `has_archive` to `true` for both `cropx_dealer` and `cropx_team_member` in `inc/cpts.php` — WordPress will never route to an `archive-{post_type}.php` template otherwise, regardless of whether the file exists. This is safe today: confirmed via the live staging Pages list (44 Pages) that no "Dealers" or "Team" Page currently exists to collide with either URL. `cropx_dealer`'s old code comment explained `has_archive` was `false` specifically to leave room for a *future* `/dealers/` Page built with the dealer-finder map block — that Page was never built. Both CPT comments were rewritten to explain the current reasoning and warn that flipping either back to `false` would be needed first if a fancier block-built Page is ever placed at `/dealers/` or `/team/` later. The dealer-finder block itself is unaffected either way — it always queries dealers via REST, never via the archive route.

**New shared stylesheet:** `styles/simple-cpt.css` — the only new CSS needed; everything else (page-title band, buttons, CTA links) reuses existing `shared.css` utilities (`.section-header`, `.pg-header`, `.btn-primary`, `.cta-link`) so these pages stay visually consistent with the rest of the site despite having no bespoke block design. Covers just the archive card grid (`.scpt-grid`/`.scpt-card`) and the single-post two-column detail layout (`.scpt-detail`). Enqueued conditionally in `inc/enqueue.php` on `is_post_type_archive()`/`is_singular()` of the three CPTs, alongside the same manually-enqueued nav JS every PHP-rendered-nav template needs (nav here is a `cropx_render_nav()` partial, not a block WordPress would auto-enqueue a viewScript for).

**Still outstanding — needs Lauren's action before these look complete on staging:** the "Fallback Cat. Pg. Demo Form + CTA" synced pattern does not exist yet (checked staging's Patterns list — 13 patterns, none named "Fallback"). Until she creates it in wp-admin (mirroring the structure of "News Cat. Pg. Demo Form + CTA" / "Insights Cat. Pg. Demo Form + CTA"), all 6 new templates will safely fall back to the plain default `cropx/pre-footer-cta` block rather than breaking — but she'll want to create it to get the intended design.

**Answered:** Lauren asked whether a template was needed for Quotes (the `cropx_testimonial` CPT). No — it's registered with `'public' => false` in `inc/cpts.php`, so WordPress never generates any front-end permalink for it; there's no URL to stumble onto.

**Delivered:** `simple-cpt-templates-deploy.zip` (6 new template files + `styles/simple-cpt.css` + updated `inc/cpts.php` + updated `inc/enqueue.php`, 9 files). No `npm run build` needed — pure PHP/CSS, nothing in `src/blocks/*` changed. PHP CLI wasn't available to lint these directly this session; verified via a manual brace/paren/bracket balance check on all 9 files instead (all balanced) — worth a normal WP File Manager smoke-test after upload given that reduced verification.

## August 24, 2026 — Root-caused and fixed: curved-hero "stale CSS" bug was WordPress's own cache-busting fallback, not our code

Lauren reported a real regression on the live curved-hero blocks right after the Aug 23 PageSpeed #7 patch: at 100% zoom a deep-blue gap appeared on the right edge of the background photo (needed ~119% zoom to hide it), and the Focal X/Focal Y sliders "seemed to do hardly anything" — she should have been able to push the image further up than the published page showed. She was clear the PageSpeed work itself should stay; she just needed the photo placement to behave like it used to.

**Diagnosis path (each step ruled something out):**
1. Fetched the deployed `style-index.css` directly for `hero-curved` — confirmed it already had the correct new `object-fit: cover; object-position: ...` rule. Server-side CSS was NOT stale.
2. Used Claude in Chrome's `javascript_tool` on the live page to read the `<img>` element's inline `style` attribute — confirmed it exactly matched Lauren's editor settings (`object-position: 100% 100%; transform: scale(1.0000); transform-origin: 100% 100%;`). The PHP/render logic was NOT at fault.
3. Read `getComputedStyle()` on that same element — the live *computed* `object-fit` was `"fill"`, not `"cover"`, and the image was rendering at its exact natural pixel size (2400×1350). This meant the correct CSS rule in `style-index.css` simply wasn't being applied to the element at all.
4. Read the actual `<link>` tag's href: `.../hero-curved/style-index.css?ver=7.1`. Ran `fetch(that_exact_url, {cache: 'no-store'})` from inside the page's own JS context — the server's response at that exact versioned URL also had the correct CSS. So the server was correct everywhere; the browser was just never re-fetching that URL.

**Root cause, confirmed by reading WordPress core source directly (`wp-includes/blocks.php` and `wp-includes/class-wp-styles.php`):** none of our `block.json` files declare a `"version"` field. WordPress's `register_block_style_handle()` only uses `metadata['version']` for the auto-registered `style`/`editorStyle` handle's cache-busting version:
```php
$block_version = ! $is_core_block && isset( $metadata['version'] ) ? $metadata['version'] : false;
```
When that's `false` (our case, on every block), `WP_Styles::do_item()` falls back to `$this->default_version` — **the WordPress core version string** (this is exactly where `?ver=7.1` comes from). That value is completely disconnected from our theme; it only changes when WordPress itself gets upgraded, never on a theme deploy. So every visitor whose browser had already cached `style-index.css?ver=7.1` before the Aug 23 patch was stuck seeing the pre-fix CSS forever — not a caching fluke, a structural bug that would have silently undermined **every future CSS/JS change to every block**, not just this one.

**Fix (`inc/enqueue.php`):** added a `block_type_metadata` filter that injects `CROPX_THEME_VERSION` as `metadata['version']` for every `cropx/*` block before it registers. This ties the auto-generated `?ver=` query string to our own theme version instead of WordPress core's version. Bumped `CROPX_THEME_VERSION` in `functions.php` from `0.2.0` → `0.2.1` to force an immediate cache-bust on this deploy.

**Important going forward:** this fix only works if `CROPX_THEME_VERSION` actually gets bumped on deploys that change block CSS/JS — it doesn't auto-detect file changes (no `filemtime()` hashing was wired in; could be a future improvement but wasn't necessary to unblock this). Documented as gotcha #10 in `CLAUDE.md`.

No `npm run build` was needed — this is a pure PHP change (a new filter + a version-constant string), nothing in `src/blocks/*` changed.

**Delivered:** `cache-busting-fix-patch.zip` (`functions.php` + `inc/enqueue.php`, 2 files, ~7KB). Because this patches root theme files rather than a block's `build/` folder, upload it into WP File Manager as two separate targeted replacements: `functions.php` at the theme root, `enqueue.php` inside `wp-content/themes/cropx/inc/`. After it's live, a hard refresh (or even just navigating to the page fresh) should immediately show the correct photo placement — but the real point of this fix is that it also protects every *other* visitor and every *future* deploy, not just this one page load.

## August 23, 2026 — PageSpeed fix #7: hero photos converted from CSS background-image to real `<img fetchpriority="high">`

Continuing the same PageSpeed pass (see the entry directly below for #1/#3/#4/#5), fix #7 addressed: "LCP image not preloadable — the hero photo is set as a CSS `background-image` rather than a real `<img>` tag, so the browser can't discover and start loading it early." Offered Lauren the cheap fix (a `<link rel="preload">` hint) against the more correct one (convert to a real `<img>`); she chose the real conversion.

Converted all 6 hero-family blocks — `hero-curved-standard`, `hero-curved`, `hero-blog`, `hero`, `segment-hero`, `hero-curved-animated` (3 live in patterns/templates, 3 currently dormant but fixed for consistency). Same pattern applied to each: the photo's wrapper div (`.shc-bg` / `.hero-bg` / `.sgh-bg`) changed from painting the photo via `background-image` to a clipping wrapper (`overflow: hidden`, since the zoom control can scale past 100%) holding a real `<img src="..." fetchpriority="high" decoding="async">`; the focal-point + zoom controls, previously `background-position` + `transform: scale()`, ported to `object-fit: cover` + `object-position` + the same `transform: scale()`/`transform-origin` applied directly to the `<img>`. `edit.js` rebuilt to match (CLAUDE.md gotcha #5) with the identical wrapper+img JSX. `hero-curved-animated` was flagged going in as the highest-risk block (it has its own device/phone pair slideshow animation) but investigation confirmed the slideshow only animates the device/phone PNG layers — the background photo itself was always static in that block too, so the same conversion applied cleanly with no interaction with the animation timing.

One per-block wrinkle: `hero` uniquely collects a real, populated `backgroundImageAlt` attribute (auto-filled from the WP media library when an editor selects an image), so its new `<img>` uses that as its real `alt` text instead of `alt=""` like the other five (which never collected alt text for this decorative photo).

Verified via a full theme build: every block's compiled `render.php` contains the new `<img fetchpriority="high">` markup with zero leftover `background-image: url(...)` for the photo layer, and compiled CSS has the new `.xxx-bg img { object-fit: cover; ... }` rule.

**Delivered:** `pagespeed-hero-img-patch.zip` (`build/blocks/hero-curved-standard/`, `build/blocks/hero-curved/`, `build/blocks/hero-blog/`, `build/blocks/hero/`, `build/blocks/segment-hero/`, `build/blocks/hero-curved-animated/` — 6 block folders, ~94KB). Small targeted patch, upload directly into WP File Manager per the project's standard deploy workflow.
